⬜ C. Use the Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS) Standard-Infrequent Access (Standard-IA) storage class. Activate the infrequent access lifecycle policy.
⬜ D. Use the Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS) One Zone-Infrequent Access (One Zone-IA) storage class. Activate the infrequent access lifecycle policy.

Explanation:
The most cost-effective solution to migrate NFS-based on-premises storage to AWS is to use AWS Storage Gateway - S3 File Gateway:
● It allows your applications to access Amazon S3 objects as NFS file shares, appearing like a local file system.
● You can apply S3 Lifecycle policies to automatically move older data to lower-cost storage classes like S3 Standard-IA, Glacier Instant Retrieval, or Deep Archive.
● This solution provides scalability, durability, and low cost, while preserving NFS compatibility.
Why other options are wrong:
A. Volume Gateway: Volume Gateway presents block storage (iSCSI) — not NFS file system — so it does not match NFS needs.
C. EFS Standard-IA: EFS is great but much more expensive compared to S3-based storage for massive, less frequently accessed data.
D. EFS One Zone-IA: Slightly cheaper than Standard-IA but still more costly than an S3-backed Storage Gateway for large, cold datasets.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/filegateway/latest/files3/WhatIsFileGateway.html

A company uses AWS Organizations to run workloads within multiple AWS accounts. A tagging policy adds department tags to AWS resources when the company creates tags.

An accounting team needs to determine spending on Amazon EC2 consumption. The accounting team must determine which departments are responsible for the costs regardless of AWS account. The accounting team has access to AWS Cost Explorer for all AWS accounts within the organization and needs to access all reports from Cost Explorer.

Which solution meets these requirements in the MOST operationally efficient way?

✅ A. From the Organizations management account billing console, activate a user-defined cost allocation tag named department. Create one cost report in Cost Explorer grouping by tag name, and filter by EC2.
⬜ B. From the Organizations management account billing console, activate an AWS-defined cost allocation tag named department. Create one cost report in Cost Explorer grouping by tag name, and filter by EC2.
⬜ C. From the Organizations member account billing console, activate a user-defined cost allocation tag named department. Create one cost report in Cost Explorer grouping by the tag name, and filter by EC2.
⬜ D. From the Organizations member account billing console, activate an AWS-defined cost allocation tag named department. Create one cost report in Cost Explorer grouping by tag name and filter by EC2.

Explanation:
The correct and most efficient solution is:
● In the management account’s billing console, you must activate the user-defined cost allocation tag (department).
● After activation, you can group by the department tag and filter by Amazon EC2 usage inside Cost Explorer.
● Only the management account has the ability to activate tags for consolidated billing across all accounts.
Why other options are wrong:
B. AWS-defined tags are automatically created by AWS for resources (e.g., createdBy), not custom department tags created by users.
C. Member accounts cannot activate tags for consolidated billing across all accounts; activation must happen from the management account.
D. Same problem as C, plus it wrongly assumes department is an AWS-defined tag.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awsaccountbilling/latest/aboutv2/cost-alloc-tags.html

A company runs a website that stores images of historical events. Website users need the ability to search and view images based on the year that the event in the image occurred. On average, users request each image only once or twice a year. The company wants a highly available solution to store and deliver the images to users.

Which solution will meet these requirements MOST cost-effectively?

⬜ A. Store images in Amazon Elastic Block Store (Amazon EBS). Use a web server that runs on Amazon EC2.
⬜ B. Store images in Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS). Use a web server that runs on Amazon EC2.
⬜ C. Store images in Amazon S3 Standard. Use S3 Standard to directly deliver images by using a static website.
✅ D. Store images in Amazon S3 Standard-Infrequent Access (S3 Standard-IA). Use S3 Standard-IA to directly deliver images by using a static website.

Explanation:
The most cost-effective solution for infrequently accessed images is Amazon S3 Standard-IA.
● S3 Standard-IA offers lower storage costs compared to S3 Standard, with slightly higher retrieval costs — ideal for objects accessed only a few times per year.
● Hosting the images using S3 static website hosting provides high availability without the need for managing servers.
Why other options are wrong:
A. EBS volumes are attached to EC2 instances and are not highly available across multiple Availability Zones without complex setups.
B. EFS is designed for frequent access, and is more expensive for infrequent access scenarios.
C. S3 Standard is highly available but more expensive than S3 Standard-IA when access frequency is low.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/storage-class-intro.html#sc-diff-ia

A company is planning to use an Amazon DynamoDB table for data storage. The company is concerned about cost optimization. The table will not be used on most mornings. In the evenings, the read and write traffic will often be unpredictable. When traffic spikes occur, they will happen very quickly.

What should a solutions architect recommend?

✅ A. Create a DynamoDB table in on-demand capacity mode.
⬜ B. Create a DynamoDB table with a global secondary index.
⬜ C. Create a DynamoDB table with provisioned capacity and auto scaling.
⬜ D. Create a DynamoDB table in provisioned capacity mode, and configure it as a global table.

Explanation:
The on-demand capacity mode for DynamoDB is ideal for:
● Unpredictable workloads with irregular traffic patterns.
● Automatic scaling to handle sudden traffic spikes without manual capacity planning.
● Cost optimization because you only pay for the reads and writes you use, without overprovisioning.
This makes on-demand mode the best fit for unpredictable, spike-prone, and cost-sensitive scenarios.
Why other options are wrong:
B. A global secondary index (GSI) helps in querying but does not address cost optimization or capacity scaling.
C. Provisioned capacity with auto scaling helps with some variability but may not scale fast enough for sudden spikes.
D. Global tables are for multi-Region replication, not for handling unpredictable traffic or cost optimization within a single Region.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/HowItWorks.ReadWriteCapacityMode.html

A business’s backup data totals 700 terabytes (TB) and is kept in network-attached storage (NAS) at its data center. This backup data must be available in the event of occasional regulatory inquiries and preserved for a period of seven years. The organization has chosen to relocate its backup data from its on-premises data center to Amazon Web Services (AWS). Within one month, the migration must be completed. The company’s public internet connection provides 500 Mbps of dedicated capacity for data transport.

What should a solutions architect do to ensure that data is migrated and stored at the LOWEST possible cost?

✅ A. Order AWS Snowball devices to transfer the data. Use a lifecycle policy to transition the files to Amazon S3 Glacier Deep Archive.
⬜ B. Deploy a VPN connection between the data center and Amazon VPC. Use the AWS CLI to copy the data from on premises to Amazon S3 Glacier.
⬜ C. Provision a 500 Mbps AWS Direct Connect connection and transfer the data to Amazon S3. Use a lifecycle policy to transition the files to Amazon S3 Glacier Deep Archive.
⬜ D. Use AWS DataSync to transfer the data and deploy a DataSync agent on premises. Use the DataSync task to copy files from the on-premises NAS storage to Amazon S3 Glacier.

Explanation:
The best option for migrating 700 TB of data within one month and at the lowest cost is to:
● A. Use AWS Snowball devices, which are designed for large-scale data migrations without depending on network bandwidth.
● After ingestion into S3, S3 lifecycle policies can automatically transition data to S3 Glacier Deep Archive for low-cost long-term storage.
Transferring 700 TB over a 500 Mbps link would take many months, not meet the one-month timeline, and incur high data transfer costs.
Why other options are wrong:
B. VPN connection over public internet would be too slow and is not intended for large bulk data migrations.
C. Provisioning Direct Connect might improve speed but requires weeks to set up, costs more, and may not meet the one-month deadline.
D. AWS DataSync uses network bandwidth, and 700 TB over a 500 Mbps line would not complete in time.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/snowball/latest/ug/whatissnowball.html

A solutions architect at an ecommerce company wants to back up application log data to Amazon S3. The solutions architect is unsure how frequently the logs will be accessed or which logs will be accessed the most. The company wants to keep costs as low as possible by using the appropriate S3 storage class.

Which S3 storage class should be implemented to meet these requirements?

⬜ A. S3 Glacier
✅ B. S3 Intelligent-Tiering
⬜ C. S3 Standard-Infrequent Access (S3 Standard-IA)
⬜ D. S3 One Zone-Infrequent Access (S3 One Zone-IA)

Explanation:
The best choice when access patterns are unknown and cost savings are needed is:
● B. S3 Intelligent-Tiering automatically moves objects between two access tiers (frequent and infrequent access) based on changing access patterns without performance impact or operational overhead.
● It is cost-optimized for data with unknown or unpredictable access patterns, and avoids unnecessary retrieval or transition fees.
Why other options are wrong:
A. S3 Glacier is for archival storage — not suitable for data that may need quick or unpredictable access.
C. S3 Standard-IA requires objects to be accessed infrequently, and early retrieval or minimum storage charges apply if accessed too soon.
D. S3 One Zone-IA stores data in a single AZ — it’s cheaper but less resilient, and still best for rarely accessed data, not uncertain access.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/intelligent-tiering-overview.html

A company runs an application on a group of Amazon Linux EC2 instances. For compliance reasons, the company must retain all application log files for 7 years. The log files will be analyzed by a reporting tool that must be able to access all the files concurrently.

Which storage solution meets these requirements MOST cost-effectively?

⬜ A. Amazon Elastic Block Store (Amazon EBS)
⬜ B. Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS)
⬜ C. Amazon EC2 instance store
✅ D. Amazon S3

Explanation:
The most cost-effective and scalable solution for long-term storage with concurrent access is:
● D. Amazon S3.
● S3 provides durable object storage with virtually unlimited capacity, and multiple clients (such as EC2 instances or reporting tools) can access objects concurrently.
● S3 is far cheaper for long-term storage compared to EBS or EFS, and it supports storage class tiers (e.g., S3 Glacier for archival) for additional cost optimization.
Why other options are wrong:
A. EBS volumes are block storage attached to a single EC2 instance and are not shared across instances without complex setup (and costlier for 7 years of storage).
B. EFS is shared file storage but is more expensive than S3 and better suited for frequent access.
C. Instance store is ephemeral and data is lost if the instance stops or terminates — unsuitable for compliance and long-term retention.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/Welcome.html

A company is running an SMB file server in its data center. The file server stores large files that are accessed frequently for the first few days after the files are created. After 7 days, the files are rarely accessed.

The total data size is increasing and is close to the company’s total storage capacity. A solutions architect must increase the company’s available storage space without losing low-latency access to the most recently accessed files. The solutions architect must also provide file lifecycle management to avoid future storage issues.

Which solution will meet these requirements?

⬜ A. Use AWS DataSync to copy data that is older than 7 days from the SMB file server to AWS.
✅ B. Create an Amazon S3 File Gateway to extend the company’s storage space. Create an S3 Lifecycle policy to transition the data to S3 Glacier Deep Archive after 7 days.
⬜ C. Create an Amazon FSx for Windows File Server file system to extend the company’s storage space.
⬜ D. Install a utility on each user’s computer to access Amazon S3. Create an S3 Lifecycle policy to transition the data to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval after 7 days.

Explanation:
The correct solution that extends storage, preserves low-latency access for new files, and automates lifecycle management is:
● B. Use an Amazon S3 File Gateway to provide on-premises SMB access to files stored in Amazon S3, while using local caching for recently accessed files.
● Additionally, an S3 Lifecycle policy can automatically transition older files to S3 Glacier Deep Archive to save costs on infrequently accessed data.
Why other options are wrong:
A. AWS DataSync copies data but does not extend the local file system or provide ongoing, seamless access.
C. Amazon FSx is a fully managed file system but would require migrating the workload and does not integrate with the existing on-premises server easily.
D. Installing a utility on each user’s computer adds complexity and breaks the SMB user experience — not a seamless extension.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/filegateway/latest/files3/WhatIsStorageGateway.html

A company uses Amazon EC2 Reserved Instances to run its data processing workload. The nightly job typically takes 7 hours to run and must finish within a 10-hour time window. The company anticipates temporary increases in demand at the end of each month that will cause the job to run over the time limit with the capacity of the current resources. Once started, the processing job cannot be interrupted before completion. The company wants to implement a solution that would provide increased resource capacity as cost-effectively as possible.

What should a solutions architect do to accomplish this?

✅ A. Deploy On-Demand Instances during periods of high demand.
⬜ B. Create a second EC2 reservation for additional instances.
⬜ C. Deploy Spot Instances during periods of high demand.
⬜ D. Increase the EC2 instance size in the EC2 reservation to support the increased workload.

Explanation:
The best approach to cost-effectively and reliably scale temporarily without risking interruption is:
● A. Use On-Demand Instances during periods of temporary high demand.
● On-Demand Instances are not interruptible (unlike Spot Instances) and provide flexible scaling without needing long-term commitments (like new Reserved Instances).
● Since the job cannot be interrupted once started, Spot Instances are not suitable despite being cheaper.
Why other options are wrong:
B. Purchasing another Reserved Instance is not cost-effective for temporary needs — Reserved Instances require a long-term commitment (1 or 3 years).
C. Spot Instances are interruptible at any time by AWS, which risks job failure.
D. Increasing the instance size would increase costs permanently — and the workload spike is temporary.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/ec2-instance-purchasing-options.html

Which of the following is not a category in AWS Trusted Advisor service checks?

⬜ A. Cost Optimization
⬜ B. Fault Tolerance
⬜ C. Service Limits
✅ D. Network Optimization

Explanation:
● D is correct because Network Optimization is not a category in AWS Trusted Advisor. The main categories that Trusted Advisor covers are Cost Optimization, Performance, Security, Fault Tolerance, and Service Limits.
● Trusted Advisor helps customers reduce costs, increase performance, improve security, and monitor service limits, but Network Optimization is not a specific category.
Why other options are wrong:
A. Cost Optimization — This is a core category in Trusted Advisor, helping to identify unused or underutilized resources.
B. Fault Tolerance — Trusted Advisor checks for things like backup, redundancy, and disaster recovery, making this an important category.
C. Service Limits — Trusted Advisor monitors service usage against AWS service limits to prevent disruptions.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awssupport/latest/user/trusted-advisor.html

Which of the following AWS services offers the most cost-effective solution for storing infrequently accessed data over a long period?

⬜ A. Amazon EBS
⬜ B. Amazon S3 Standard
✅ C. Amazon S3 Glacier
⬜ D. Amazon RDS

Explanation:
C. Amazon S3 Glacier is designed for long-term, infrequently accessed data storage at a very low cost. It is ideal for archival and backup use cases where retrieval times of minutes to hours are acceptable.
Why other options are wrong:
A. Amazon EBS is block storage optimized for low-latency access and is relatively expensive for infrequent access scenarios.
B. Amazon S3 Standard is optimized for frequent access to data, making it costlier than S3 Glacier for long-term storage.
D. Amazon RDS is a managed relational database service, intended for active databases and not suitable or cost-effective for simple, infrequently accessed data storage.
Source: https://aws.amazon.com/s3/storage-classes/

A Solutions Architect is designing a web application that experiences seasonal traffic. To optimize costs, which combination of AWS services should be used for compute resources? (Select TWO.)

✅ A. Amazon EC2 On-Demand Instances
⬜ B. Amazon EC2 Reserved Instances
✅ C. AWS Lambda
⬜ D. Amazon EC2 Spot Instances
⬜ E. Amazon EC2 Dedicated Hosts

Explanation:
● Amazon EC2 On-Demand Instances are flexible for fluctuating seasonal workloads without long-term commitment, ideal for handling unexpected spikes.
● AWS Lambda is a serverless option that automatically scales with demand, and you only pay per invocation, making it highly cost-efficient for unpredictable or seasonal traffic.
Why other options are wrong:
B. Amazon EC2 Reserved Instances are cost-effective for steady, predictable workloads, but not ideal for seasonal traffic patterns.
D. Amazon EC2 Spot Instances are cheaper but can be terminated with short notice, which might not be acceptable for a web application needing reliable availability.
E. Amazon EC2 Dedicated Hosts are intended for licensing and compliance needs, not cost optimization for seasonal workloads.
Source: https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/pricing/

A Solutions Architect is designing a cost-optimized architecture for a workload that has predictable, consistent performance requirements. Which EC2 purchasing option would be the most cost-effective?

⬜ A. On-Demand Instances
⬜ B. Spot Instances
✅ C. Reserved Instances
⬜ D. Dedicated Hosts

Explanation:
C. Reserved Instances provide a significant discount compared to On-Demand pricing when you commit to using Amazon EC2 over a one-year or three-year term. They are ideal for workloads with predictable, steady usage patterns, making them the most cost-effective choice in this scenario.
Why other options are wrong:
A. On-Demand Instances are better suited for short-term, irregular workloads, and are more expensive for steady, predictable usage.
B. Spot Instances offer the lowest cost but are designed for flexible workloads that can handle interruptions, not predictable workloads requiring consistent performance.
D. Dedicated Hosts provide physical servers for licensing or compliance needs, but are usually more costly and are not the best option just for cost-optimization.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/ri-overview.html

A company has a financial application that produces reports. The reports average 50 KB in size and are stored in Amazon S3. The reports are frequently accessed during the first week after production and must be stored for several years. The reports must be retrievable within 6 hours. Which solution meets these requirements MOST cost-effectively?

✅ A. Use S3 Standard. Use an S3 Lifecycle rule to transition the reports to S3 Glacier after 7 days.
⬜ B. Use S3 Standard. Use an S3 Lifecycle rule to transition the reports to S3 Standard-Infrequent Access (S3 Standard-IA) after 7 days.
⬜ C. Use S3 Intelligent-Tiering. Configure S3 Intelligent-Tiering to transition the reports to S3 Standard-Infrequent Access (S3 Standard-IA) and S3 Glacier.
⬜ D. Use S3 Standard. Use an S3 Lifecycle rule to transition the reports to S3 Glacier Deep Archive after 7 days.

Explanation:
● S3 Glacier is a cost-effective storage class for data that is infrequently accessed but needs to be retrieved within hours. Since the requirement specifies a retrieval time of within 6 hours, S3 Glacier is ideal. Transitioning after 7 days when access drops is also a good optimization.
Why other options are wrong:
B. S3 Standard-IA is less costly than S3 Standard but is more expensive compared to Glacier for long-term storage needs.
C. S3 Intelligent-Tiering charges monitoring and automation fees; it’s better for unpredictable access patterns, not for known predictable patterns like in this case.
D. S3 Glacier Deep Archive has a retrieval time of up to 12 hours, which exceeds the 6-hour requirement.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations.html

A company runs several Amazon RDS for Oracle On-Demand DB instances that have high utilization. The RDS DB instances run in member accounts that are in an organization in AWS Organizations.

The company’s finance team has access to the organization’s management account and member accounts. The finance team wants to find ways to optimize costs by using AWS Trusted Advisor.

Which combination of steps will meet these requirements? (Choose two.)

✅ A. Use the Trusted Advisor recommendations in the management account.
⬜ B. Use the Trusted Advisor recommendations in the member accounts where the RDS DB instances are running.
✅ C. Review the Trusted Advisor checks for Amazon RDS Reserved Instance Optimization.
⬜ D. Review the Trusted Advisor checks for Amazon RDS Idle DB Instances.
⬜ E. Review the Trusted Advisor checks for compute optimization. Crosscheck the results by using AWS Compute Optimizer.

Explanation:
● A is correct because the management account can aggregate and view Trusted Advisor recommendations for all member accounts when using AWS Organizations.
● C is correct because the Amazon RDS Reserved Instance Optimization check in Trusted Advisor suggests opportunities to purchase Reserved Instances based on historical usage patterns, helping optimize costs.
Why other options are wrong:
B. Using Trusted Advisor individually in each member account is not necessary when centralized management through the management account is available.
D. “Idle DB Instances” checks are useful for unused resources, but the question mentions high utilization, making Reserved Instance recommendations more relevant.
E. Compute Optimizer primarily analyzes EC2, Auto Scaling, and Lambda, not directly RDS. Hence, RDS optimization is not covered by Compute Optimizer.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awssupport/latest/user/ta-best-practices.html

A company runs its customer-facing web application on containers. The workload uses Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS) on AWS Fargate. The web application is resource intensive.

The web application needs to be available 24 hours a day, 7 days a week for customers. The company expects the application to experience short bursts of high traffic. The workload must be highly available.

Which solution will meet these requirements MOST cost-effectively?

⬜ A. Configure an ECS capacity provider with Fargate. Conduct load testing by using a third-party tool. Rightsize the Fargate tasks in Amazon CloudWatch.
✅ B. Configure an ECS capacity provider with Fargate for steady state and Fargate Spot for burst traffic.
⬜ C. Configure an ECS capacity provider with Fargate Spot for steady state and Fargate for burst traffic.
⬜ D. Configure an ECS capacity provider with Fargate. Use AWS Compute Optimizer to rightsize the Fargate task.

Explanation:
● B is correct because steady workloads should use regular Fargate to ensure high availability and reliability, while burst traffic can be handled by Fargate Spot, which is cheaper but can be interrupted. This provides cost optimization without sacrificing availability.
Why other options are wrong:
A. Load testing and rightsizing are good practices but do not address handling burst traffic cost-effectively with Spot capacity.
C. Using Fargate Spot for steady-state workloads risks interruptions and availability issues, which is not acceptable for a 24/7 application.
D. AWS Compute Optimizer helps with rightsizing, but it doesn’t solve cost optimization for burst traffic like Fargate Spot does.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonECS/latest/developerguide/using-fargate-capacity-providers.html

A company is running a custom application on Amazon EC2 On-Demand Instances. The application has frontend nodes that need to run 24 hours a day, 7 days a week, and backend nodes that need to run only for a short time based on workload. The number of backend nodes varies during the day.
</br/>The company needs to scale out and scale in more instances based on workload.
</br/>Which solution will meet these requirements MOST cost-effectively?

⬜ A. Use Reserved Instances for the frontend nodes. Use AWS Fargate for the backend nodes.
✅ B. Use Reserved Instances for the frontend nodes. Use Spot Instances for the backend nodes.
⬜ C. Use Spot Instances for the frontend nodes. Use Reserved Instances for the backend nodes.
⬜ D. Use Spot Instances for the frontend nodes. Use AWS Fargate for the backend nodes.

Explanation:
● Reserved Instances provide cost savings for predictable, 24/7 workloads such as frontend nodes.
● Spot Instances are ideal for backend nodes that are short-lived and scalable based on fluctuating demand, making them highly cost-effective.
● Auto Scaling can be used with Spot Instances to dynamically scale based on demand while minimizing cost.
Why other options are wrong:
A. AWS Fargate is intended for containerized workloads, not EC2-based ones. It’s generally more expensive than Spot Instances for dynamic workloads.
C. Spot Instances are unsuitable for the always-on frontend nodes due to possible interruptions.
D. Similar to A and C—using Spot for frontend is unreliable, and Fargate adds unnecessary cost for backend EC2-based processing.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/using-instances-reserved.html
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/using-spot-instances.html

A company runs a production application on a fleet of Amazon EC2 instances. The application reads the data from an Amazon SQS queue and processes the messages in parallel. The message volume is unpredictable and often has intermittent traffic. This application should continually process messages without any downtime.

Which solution meets these requirements MOST cost-effectively?

⬜ A. Use Spot Instances exclusively to handle the maximum capacity required.
⬜ B. Use Reserved Instances exclusively to handle the maximum capacity required.
✅ C. Use Reserved Instances for the baseline capacity and use Spot Instances to handle additional capacity.
⬜ D. Use Reserved Instances for the baseline capacity and use On-Demand Instances to handle additional capacity.

Explanation:
● Reserved Instances (RIs) are cost-effective for baseline, predictable usage, providing significant savings over On-Demand.
● Spot Instances are ideal for handling intermittent or burst workloads, like processing spikes from an SQS queue, at a fraction of the cost.
● Combining both allows the application to run continuously with cost efficiency and scale during unpredictable spikes.
Why other options are wrong:
A. Spot Instances can be interrupted, which risks downtime in a production environment.
B. Reserved Instances for the full capacity would lead to overprovisioning and increased cost due to unpredictable traffic.
D. Using On-Demand Instances for spikes ensures availability but is less cost-effective compared to Spot Instances.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/using-instances-spot.html
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/ec2-reserved-instances.html

A company recently launched a new application for its customers. The application runs on multiple Amazon EC2 instances across two Availability Zones. End users use TCP to communicate with the application.

The application must be highly available and must automatically scale as the number of users increases.

Which combination of steps will meet these requirements MOST cost-effectively? (Choose two.)

✅ A. Add a Network Load Balancer in front of the EC2 instances.
✅ B. Configure an Auto Scaling group for the EC2 instances.
⬜ C. Add an Application Load Balancer in front of the EC2 instances.
⬜ D. Manually add more EC2 instances for the application.
⬜ E. Add a Gateway Load Balancer in front of the EC2 instances.

Explanation:
● A. A Network Load Balancer (NLB) is best suited for TCP traffic and provides high performance and low latency at a lower cost, making it appropriate for this use case.
● B. Auto Scaling groups allow EC2 instances to scale automatically in and out based on demand, ensuring cost-efficiency and high availability across multiple Availability Zones.
Why other options are wrong:
C. Application Load Balancers are ideal for HTTP/HTTPS traffic at Layer 7, not for TCP-based applications.
D. Manually adding instances is not scalable or cost-efficient and does not meet automatic scaling requirements.
E. Gateway Load Balancers are typically used for third-party virtual appliances such as firewalls, not for balancing TCP application traffic.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/elasticloadbalancing/latest/network/introduction.html Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/autoscaling/ec2/userguide/what-is-amazon-ec2-auto-scaling.html

A company hosts a website analytics application on a single Amazon EC2 On-Demand Instance. The analytics software is written in PHP and uses a MySQL database. The analytics software, the web server that provides PHP, and the database server are all hosted on the EC2 instance. The application is showing signs of performance degradation during busy times and is presenting 5xx errors. The company needs to make the application scale seamlessly.

Which solution will meet these requirements MOST cost-effectively?

⬜ A. Migrate the database to an Amazon RDS for MySQL DB instance. Create an AMI of the web application. Use the AMI to launch a second EC2 On-Demand Instance. Use an Application Load Balancer to distribute the load to each EC2 instance.
⬜ B. Migrate the database to an Amazon RDS for MySQL DB instance. Create an AMI of the web application. Use the AMI to launch a second EC2 On-Demand Instance. Use Amazon Route 53 weighted routing to distribute the load across the two EC2 instances.
⬜ C. Migrate the database to an Amazon Aurora MySQL DB instance. Create an AWS Lambda function to stop the EC2 instance and change the instance type. Create an Amazon CloudWatch alarm to invoke the Lambda function when CPU utilization surpasses 75%.
✅ D. Migrate the database to an Amazon Aurora MySQL DB instance. Create an AMI of the web application. Apply the AMI to a launch template. Create an Auto Scaling group with the launch template. Configure the launch template to use a Spot Fleet. Attach an Application Load Balancer to the Auto Scaling group.

Explanation:
D. This option offers high availability and scalability with the use of Amazon Aurora (a fully managed, scalable, and fault-tolerant database) and EC2 Auto Scaling. Using a Spot Fleet further optimizes cost, while the Application Load Balancer ensures that traffic is evenly distributed among available instances, making it the most cost-effective and scalable solution.
Why other options are wrong:
A. This solution uses only two fixed On-Demand instances, which does not scale automatically based on demand and is more expensive than Spot.
B. Similar to A, it lacks automatic scaling, and Route 53 weighted routing does not provide health checks and failover capabilities as efficiently as an ALB.
C. Changing the EC2 instance type via Lambda and CloudWatch does not support horizontal scaling and could cause downtime or delays during resizing.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/autoscaling/ec2/userguide/what-is-amazon-ec2-auto-scaling.html Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonRDS/latest/AuroraUserGuide/CHAP_AuroraOverview.html

A company is launching an application on AWS. The application uses an Application Load Balancer (ALB) to direct traffic to at least two Amazon EC2 instances in a single target group. The instances are in an Auto Scaling group for each environment. The company requires a development environment and a production environment. The production environment will have periods of high traffic.

Which solution will configure the development environment MOST cost-effectively?

⬜ A. Reconfigure the target group in the development environment to have only one EC2 instance as a target.
⬜ B. Change the ALB balancing algorithm to least outstanding requests.
⬜ C. Reduce the size of the EC2 instances in both environments.
✅ D. Reduce the maximum number of EC2 instances in the development environment’s Auto Scaling group.

Explanation:
● D. Reducing the maximum number of instances in the development Auto Scaling group allows the system to scale only as needed within a controlled cost boundary. Since development environments typically require fewer resources, this is a cost-effective and scalable solution.
Why other options are wrong:
A. Reconfiguring to only one instance removes high availability and is not aligned with AWS best practices.
B. Changing the ALB algorithm does not impact cost — it only affects how requests are distributed.
C. Reducing instance size in both environments may degrade production performance, which is not acceptable given high traffic expectations.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/autoscaling/ec2/userguide/asg-scaling-options.html
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/elasticloadbalancing/latest/application/introduction.html

A company runs its application by using Amazon EC2 instances and AWS Lambda functions. The EC2 instances run in private subnets of a VPC. The Lambda functions need direct network access to the EC2 instances for the application to work.

The application will run for 1 year. The number of Lambda functions that the application uses will increase during the 1-year period. The company must minimize costs on all application resources.

Which solution will meet these requirements?

⬜ A. Purchase an EC2 Instance Savings Plan. Connect the Lambda functions to the private subnets that contain the EC2 instances.
⬜ B. Purchase an EC2 Instance Savings Plan. Connect the Lambda functions to new public subnets in the same VPC where the EC2 instances run.
✅ C. Purchase a Compute Savings Plan. Connect the Lambda functions to the private subnets that contain the EC2 instances.
⬜ D. Purchase a Compute Savings Plan. Keep the Lambda functions in the Lambda service VPC.

Explanation:
● C is correct because Compute Savings Plans apply to both EC2 instances and AWS Lambda, providing the most cost-effective option when both services are in use.
● Connecting Lambda functions to the private subnets that host the EC2 instances allows for direct VPC access, fulfilling the requirement for Lambda-to-EC2 communication.
Why other options are wrong:
A. EC2 Instance Savings Plans only apply to specific EC2 instance types and do not apply to Lambda, missing out on Lambda cost savings.
B. Placing Lambda functions in public subnets is unnecessary and incorrect for accessing EC2 instances in private subnets.
D. Lambda functions in the default service VPC cannot access private EC2 subnets directly, which violates the connectivity requirement.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/savingsplans/latest/userguide/compute-savings-plans.html
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/lambda/latest/dg/configuration-vpc.html

A company is launching a new application that requires a structured database to store user profiles, application settings, and transactional data. The database must be scalable with application traffic and must offer backups.

Which solution will meet these requirements MOST cost-effectively?

⬜ A. Deploy a self-managed database on Amazon EC2 instances by using open source software. Use Spot Instances for cost optimization. Configure automated backups to Amazon S3.
⬜ B. Use Amazon RDS. Use on-demand capacity mode for the database with General Purpose SSD storage. Configure automatic backups with a retention period of 7 days.
✅ C. Use Amazon Aurora Serverless for the database. Use serverless capacity scaling. Configure automated backups to Amazon S3.
⬜ D. Deploy a self-managed NoSQL database on Amazon EC2 instances. Use Reserved Instances for cost optimization. Configure automated backups directly to Amazon S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval.

Explanation:
● Amazon Aurora Serverless is a fully managed, auto-scaling, on-demand relational database option that automatically starts up, shuts down, and scales based on application needs.
● It supports structured relational data, is cost-effective for variable workloads, and automatically backs up to Amazon S3, meeting both scalability and backup requirements with minimal operational overhead.
Why other options are wrong:
A. Running a self-managed database on EC2, even with Spot Instances, increases operational burden and risks due to Spot interruptions.
B. RDS with on-demand mode provides scalability but is less cost-effective than Aurora Serverless for unpredictable workloads.
D. A self-managed NoSQL solution doesn’t meet the requirement for a structured database and adds unnecessary complexity.
Source: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonRDS/latest/AuroraUserGuide/aurora-serverless.html

A company runs a self-managed application on a single Amazon EC2 instance. During a scheduled maintenance window, an engineer needs to stop the instance for a few hours and then start it again later the same day.

Which statement correctly describes the billing impact of stopping (not terminating) the EC2 instance?

⬜ A. The company stops paying for both compute and the attached Amazon EBS volumes as soon as the instance is stopped.
✅ B. The company stops paying for instance compute usage while stopped, but continues to pay for the attached Amazon EBS volumes.
⬜ C. The company continues to pay full compute and storage charges until the instance is explicitly terminated.
⬜ D. AWS automatically deletes the instance’s root EBS volume as soon as the instance is stopped, so no further storage charges apply.

Explanation:
When an EBS-backed EC2 instance is stopped, AWS stops billing for instance (compute) usage, but the attached EBS volumes remain provisioned and continue to incur standard EBS storage charges until the volumes are deleted. Data on the volumes is preserved and the instance can be started again later with that data intact.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Stopping an instance does not stop EBS storage billing — only compute billing stops.
Option C: Full compute charges do not continue while an instance is stopped; only storage charges continue.
Option D: Stopping an instance does not delete the root volume. Deletion of the root volume on stop only happens if you explicitly terminate the instance with ‘Delete on Termination’ enabled (default for the root volume), not on a simple stop.
Source: Instance lifecycle (stop, start, hibernate, terminate)

A DevOps team needs to move 500 GB of data from an Amazon EC2 instance to an Amazon S3 bucket. Both the EC2 instance and the S3 bucket are in the same AWS Region.

Which statement correctly describes the data transfer charges for this operation?

⬜ A. The company is charged standard data transfer OUT rates for every GB transferred from EC2 to S3.
✅ B. The company is charged only for the S3 PUT requests; there is no data transfer charge for moving data from EC2 to S3 within the same Region.
⬜ C. The company is charged double the standard data transfer rate because the data crosses two separate services.
⬜ D. The company is charged data transfer fees only if the S3 bucket uses S3 Standard-Infrequent Access storage class.

Explanation:
Data transferred from an Amazon EC2 instance to an Amazon S3 bucket in the same AWS Region does not incur a data transfer charge. The only charges that apply are the standard S3 request charges (e.g., PUT/COPY/POST/LIST requests) and any applicable S3 storage charges — there’s no separate EC2-to-S3 transfer fee when both resources are in the same Region.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Data transfer OUT charges apply to data leaving AWS to the internet or moving across Regions, not to same-Region EC2-to-S3 transfers.
Option C: There’s no ‘double charge’ concept for transfers between AWS services in the same Region.
Option D: Data transfer pricing is independent of the destination bucket’s storage class; storage class only affects storage and retrieval pricing.
Source: Amazon EC2 On-Demand Pricing — data transfer

A marketing team wants to run a one-day campaign application on Amazon EC2 to gather feedback on an upcoming product launch. The application must run without interruption for the full day, and the team wants to control costs since the workload is short-lived and non-recurring.

Which EC2 purchasing option should the team use?

⬜ A. Spot Instances, because they offer the deepest discount for short-lived workloads.
✅ B. On-Demand Instances, because the workload is short-lived, non-recurring, and must not be interrupted.
⬜ C. Standard Reserved Instances with a 1-year commitment, to lock in the lowest possible hourly rate.
⬜ D. Dedicated Hosts, to guarantee the underlying physical server is not shared with other customers.

Explanation:
On-Demand Instances are billed per second/hour with no long-term commitment and, critically, are not subject to interruption by AWS — making them the right fit for a short, one-off workload that must run without interruption for the day. Spot Instances are cheaper but can be reclaimed by AWS with a two-minute interruption notice, which conflicts with the ‘must function without interruption’ requirement.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Spot Instances can be interrupted by AWS at any time with a two-minute warning, which directly conflicts with the requirement that the application run without interruption.
Option C: A 1-year Reserved Instance commitment does not make sense for a one-day, non-recurring workload — the company would pay for capacity it doesn’t use for the rest of the term.
Option D: Dedicated Hosts address hardware isolation/licensing needs, not interruption risk or cost efficiency for a one-day workload, and cost significantly more.
Source: Determine what instance purchasing option to use

A customer needs to migrate approximately 60 petabytes of images and videos from an on-premises data center to Amazon S3. The company has limited network bandwidth and wants the most cost-effective and time-efficient way to move this volume of data.

Which AWS service should the customer use?

⬜ A. AWS Snowball Edge, using as many devices as needed in parallel.
✅ B. AWS Snowmobile.
⬜ C. Amazon S3 Transfer Acceleration.
⬜ D. Amazon VPC with a Site-to-Site VPN connection.

Explanation:
AWS guidance recommends AWS Snowmobile for exabyte-scale and very-large petabyte-scale migrations — AWS specifically suggests Snowmobile for datasets of roughly 10 PB or more, and Snowball/Snowball Edge for datasets below that threshold. At 60 PB, Snowmobile (a shipping-container-sized mobile data transfer service) is both more cost-effective and faster than coordinating dozens of individual Snowball Edge devices or relying on limited network bandwidth.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Snowball Edge is designed for smaller-scale migrations (up to petabytes); coordinating the number of devices needed for 60 PB is far less efficient than a single Snowmobile engagement.
Option C: S3 Transfer Acceleration speeds up transfers over the public internet using CloudFront edge locations, but it’s still bound by the company’s limited network bandwidth — impractical at 60 PB.
Option D: A Site-to-Site VPN adds encryption over an internet or Direct Connect path but does nothing to solve the fundamental bandwidth constraint for a 60 PB transfer.
Source: AWS Snowmobile FAQs

A company’s CIO wants to reduce the operational overhead of maintaining a relational database. The database currently runs as self-managed software on an Amazon EC2 instance, and the team spends significant time on patching, backups, and failover configuration.

Which solution will reduce operational overhead the MOST while keeping the same relational database engine?

⬜ A. Move the database to a larger EC2 instance type to reduce the frequency of maintenance windows.
✅ B. Migrate the database to Amazon RDS or Amazon Aurora for the same database engine.
⬜ C. Configure AWS Backup to take nightly snapshots of the EC2 instance’s EBS volumes.
⬜ D. Deploy a second EC2 instance in a different Availability Zone and manually replicate data between the two.

Explanation:
Amazon RDS (and Amazon Aurora, for supported engines) is a fully managed relational database service that handles patching, automated backups, and Multi-AZ failover on the customer’s behalf — directly eliminating most of the operational burden the team currently carries on a self-managed EC2 database, without requiring a database engine change.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: A larger instance type may improve performance but does nothing to reduce the operational work of patching, backups, or failover — the team still manages all of it manually.
Option C: Backups alone address only one part of operational overhead; patching and failover configuration remain fully manual.
Option D: Manually replicating data between self-managed EC2 instances increases operational complexity rather than reducing it.
Source: Amazon RDS overview

A company must retain compliance log files in Amazon S3 for seven years. The logs are accessed frequently during the first 30 days, rarely for the next 11 months, and essentially never after one year, though they must remain retrievable if requested by an auditor. The company wants the most cost-effective storage approach.

Which solution meets these requirements?

⬜ A. Store all log files in S3 Standard for the full seven-year retention period.
✅ B. Create an S3 Lifecycle rule that transitions objects to S3 Standard-IA after 30 days and to S3 Glacier Deep Archive after 365 days.
⬜ C. Store the log files only in Amazon EBS snapshots, which are cheaper than S3 for long-term retention.
⬜ D. Delete the logs after 30 days and rely on the company’s on-premises backup for the remaining retention period.

Explanation:
An S3 Lifecycle configuration that automatically transitions objects through storage classes as their access pattern changes — S3 Standard for the first 30 days (frequent access), S3 Standard-IA for the following months (infrequent access), and S3 Glacier Deep Archive after a year (rare, long-term retention) — minimizes storage cost at each stage while keeping the data retrievable to satisfy the seven-year compliance requirement.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Keeping all data in S3 Standard for seven years is far more expensive than necessary given the described access pattern.
Option C: EBS snapshots are priced for incremental backup of block volumes, not designed or optimized as a long-term compliance archive, and are more expensive than Glacier Deep Archive for this use case.
Option D: Deleting the logs after 30 days would violate the seven-year retention requirement.
Source: Amazon S3 Lifecycle

A company runs a predictable, steady-state workload on Amazon EC2 that it expects to keep running for the next three years. The company wants to minimize compute costs as much as possible and is comfortable using a consistent instance family and Region throughout that period.

Which purchasing option will provide the GREATEST cost savings compared to On-Demand pricing?

⬜ A. Spot Instances.
✅ B. A 3-year All Upfront Standard Reserved Instance (or equivalent Savings Plan commitment).
⬜ C. A 1-year No Upfront Convertible Reserved Instance.
⬜ D. On-Demand Instances with AWS Trusted Advisor cost recommendations enabled.

Explanation:
For a predictable, long-running, steady-state workload, committing to a 3-year term with an all-upfront payment option (Standard Reserved Instances or an equivalent Compute Savings Plan) provides the deepest discount off On-Demand pricing — typically the highest savings percentage among all EC2 purchasing options, in exchange for the longest commitment and largest upfront payment.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Spot Instances can offer steep discounts but are subject to interruption, making them unsuitable as the primary purchasing option for a workload that must run continuously for three years.
Option C: A 1-year Convertible RI with no upfront payment offers flexibility but a smaller discount than a 3-year all-upfront commitment.
Option D: On-Demand pricing (even with cost recommendations) remains the most expensive option per hour compared to any reserved commitment for a steady-state workload.
Source: Reserved Instances

A company runs dozens of Amazon EC2 instances and suspects that many are over-provisioned relative to their actual CPU, memory, and network utilization, resulting in unnecessary compute spend. The company wants AWS to analyze historical utilization metrics and recommend more cost-effective instance types and sizes, with minimal ongoing effort from the team.

Which AWS service should the company use?

⬜ A. AWS Cost Explorer.
✅ B. AWS Compute Optimizer.
⬜ C. Amazon CloudWatch Logs Insights.
⬜ D. AWS Trusted Advisor’s service limit checks.

Explanation:
AWS Compute Optimizer analyzes historical utilization metrics (CPU, memory when the CloudWatch agent is installed, network, and more) for EC2 instances, Auto Scaling groups, EBS volumes, and Lambda functions, and generates specific right-sizing recommendations to reduce cost while maintaining performance — with no additional infrastructure for the team to build or maintain.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: AWS Cost Explorer visualizes and forecasts spend and usage trends, but it doesn’t analyze per-resource utilization metrics to recommend specific instance type/size changes.
Option C: CloudWatch Logs Insights is a log query and analysis tool, not a resource right-sizing recommendation engine.
Option D: Trusted Advisor’s service limit checks flag when you’re approaching account service quotas — they don’t provide instance right-sizing recommendations (that specific check is a separate Trusted Advisor cost-optimization check, but Compute Optimizer is the purpose-built service for this).
Source: AWS Compute Optimizer

A company runs a fleet of Amazon EC2 instances split across two Availability Zones within the same Region and same VPC. An architect notices a recurring line item for inter-AZ data transfer on the company’s bill and wants to understand when this charge applies.

Which statement is correct?

⬜ A. Data transferred between EC2 instances in different Availability Zones of the same Region is always free, just like data transferred within the same AZ.
✅ B. Data transferred between EC2 instances in different Availability Zones of the same Region is billed per GB in each direction; data transferred between instances in the same AZ using private IP addresses is free.
⬜ C. Data transfer charges apply only when traffic crosses into a different AWS Region, never within a Region regardless of AZ.
⬜ D. Inter-AZ data transfer is free as long as an Application Load Balancer is used between the instances.

Explanation:
AWS charges for data transferred between Availability Zones within the same Region (typically per GB, in each direction), while data transferred between instances in the same Availability Zone using private IP addresses is not charged. This distinction is why architectures with heavy east-west traffic across AZs (e.g., chatty microservices) can see a meaningful inter-AZ data transfer line item, and why co-locating latency- and cost-sensitive components in the same AZ (when HA requirements allow) can reduce that cost.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Inter-AZ transfer is not free — only same-AZ private traffic is free.
Option C: Regional (inter-AZ) data transfer charges are separate from, and in addition to, any inter-Region charges.
Option D: Using an Application Load Balancer does not exempt the underlying inter-AZ data transfer from standard charges.
Source: Amazon EC2 On-Demand Pricing — data transfer within the same Region

A company takes daily full backups of a 350 TB Amazon EBS volume by creating a new EBS snapshot each day. The storage team is concerned about the growing cost of storing 30 days of these daily snapshots and asks a solutions architect to explain how EBS snapshot storage is actually billed.

Which statement correctly describes EBS snapshot billing?

⬜ A. Each snapshot is billed as a full, independent copy of the entire volume, so 30 daily snapshots of a 350 TB volume cost roughly the same as storing 30 separate 350 TB volumes.
✅ B. EBS snapshots are incremental — after the first (full) snapshot, each subsequent snapshot is billed only for the blocks that changed since the previous snapshot.
⬜ C. EBS snapshots are billed at a flat monthly rate per volume regardless of how many snapshots are taken or how much data changes.
⬜ D. EBS snapshot storage is free; the company is only billed for the compute cost of the EC2 instance the volume is attached to.

Explanation:
Amazon EBS snapshots are incremental: the first snapshot of a volume is a full copy of the data at that point in time, but every subsequent snapshot only stores the blocks that have changed since the previous snapshot. This means the storage (and cost) for 30 daily snapshots of a mostly-unchanged 350 TB volume is far smaller than 30 full copies, even though each snapshot logically represents a complete, restorable point-in-time image of the volume.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: This describes full backups, not how EBS snapshots are actually billed — incremental snapshotting is the key cost-saving behavior of the service.
Option C: Snapshot billing is based on the actual incremental storage consumed, not a flat per-volume rate.
Option D: EBS snapshot storage is billed per GB-month stored in Amazon S3-backed snapshot storage — it is not free and is separate from EC2 compute billing.
Source: Amazon EBS snapshots

A company uses AWS Backup to protect Amazon EBS volumes and Amazon RDS databases. Backups must be retained for 5 years to meet a regulatory requirement, but the backups are almost never restored after the first 90 days. The company wants to minimize backup storage cost without writing any custom automation.

Which AWS Backup feature should the company use?

⬜ A. Cross-Region backup copies to a second Region.
✅ B. A backup plan lifecycle rule that transitions recovery points to cold storage after 90 days and deletes them after 5 years.
⬜ C. AWS Backup Vault Lock in compliance mode.
⬜ D. Continuous backup for point-in-time recovery.

Explanation:
AWS Backup lifecycle rules let you define, as part of a backup plan, when a recovery point automatically transitions to lower-cost cold storage (after a minimum number of days) and when it is permanently deleted — meeting the 5-year retention requirement while minimizing storage cost during the years the backups are rarely, if ever, restored, with no custom scripting needed.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Cross-Region copies address disaster recovery/compliance geography requirements, not storage cost — and would add cost, not reduce it.
Option C: Vault Lock enforces immutability/WORM compliance on backups; it doesn’t address storage tiering or cost.
Option D: Continuous backup (point-in-time recovery) is about recovery granularity, not long-term retention cost optimization.
Source: Managing lifecycle of AWS Backup recovery points

A company stores objects in Amazon S3 whose access patterns are unpredictable — some objects are accessed frequently for a while and then go unused for months, and the company doesn’t know in advance which objects will follow which pattern. The company wants to minimize storage cost automatically without performing its own access-pattern analysis or writing custom lifecycle rules per object.

Which S3 storage class should the company use?

⬜ A. S3 Standard for all objects, regardless of access pattern.
✅ B. S3 Intelligent-Tiering.
⬜ C. S3 One Zone-IA for all objects.
⬜ D. S3 Glacier Deep Archive for all objects.

Explanation:
S3 Intelligent-Tiering automatically monitors access patterns for each object and moves objects between frequent-access and infrequent-access tiers (and optionally archive tiers) based on actual observed usage — with no retrieval fees and no need for the company to analyze access patterns itself or write custom lifecycle rules — making it the right fit when access patterns are unknown or unpredictable.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Keeping everything in S3 Standard means paying the highest per-GB storage rate even for objects that go unused for months, which is not cost-minimizing.
Option C: S3 One Zone-IA is appropriate for known infrequent-access, re-creatable data — using it for all objects (including frequently-accessed ones) would incur retrieval fees and reduced durability for data that’s actually accessed often.
Option D: Glacier Deep Archive has the lowest storage cost but the slowest retrieval (hours) and retrieval fees — using it for all objects, including ones that turn out to be frequently accessed, would be both slow and costly.
Source: Amazon S3 Intelligent-Tiering

A company runs EC2 instances in private subnets that need outbound internet access to download software updates, but no inbound access from the internet is needed. The company currently uses a NAT gateway in each of its 3 Availability Zones and wants to understand the primary cost driver of this setup, as the bill has been higher than expected.

Which factor is the PRIMARY driver of NAT gateway cost in this scenario?

⬜ A. NAT gateways are billed only a small flat monthly fee, with no charge based on usage.
✅ B. NAT gateways incur both an hourly charge per gateway AND a per-GB data processing charge for all data that passes through them — so 3 NAT gateways processing significant outbound traffic can add up quickly.
⬜ C. NAT gateways are free; the charge must be coming from EC2 compute costs instead.
⬜ D. NAT gateway cost is a one-time setup fee only, never a recurring or usage-based charge.

Explanation:
AWS charges for a NAT gateway both an hourly rate per NAT gateway that’s provisioned, and a per-GB data processing charge for all traffic that flows through it (in addition to standard data transfer rates) — so running one NAT gateway per AZ (3 total) with meaningful outbound traffic (e.g., frequent software updates across many instances) directly multiplies both the hourly and per-GB charges, which is usually the primary driver when NAT costs are higher than expected.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: NAT gateway billing includes a meaningful usage-based (per-GB processed) component, not just a flat fee.
Option C: NAT gateways are not free — they have their own distinct line item on the AWS bill, separate from EC2 compute.
Option D: NAT gateway charges are ongoing (hourly plus usage-based), not a one-time fee.
Source: NAT gateway pricing

A company runs Amazon EC2 instances in private subnets that need to access Amazon S3 and Amazon DynamoDB, but not the general internet. The company currently routes this traffic through a NAT gateway and wants to reduce both cost and reliance on the NAT gateway for this specific access pattern.

Which solution reduces cost the MOST for this specific traffic?

⬜ A. Add more NAT gateways to spread out the load.
✅ B. Configure a Gateway VPC endpoint for Amazon S3 and Amazon DynamoDB, so this traffic routes privately without going through the NAT gateway at all.
⬜ C. Increase the NAT gateway’s bandwidth allocation.
⬜ D. Move the EC2 instances into public subnets so they can reach S3 and DynamoDB directly over the internet.

Explanation:
Gateway VPC endpoints for Amazon S3 and Amazon DynamoDB allow traffic from a VPC to reach those two services over the AWS private network, entirely bypassing the NAT gateway (and the internet) — and Gateway endpoints have no hourly or per-GB data processing charge, unlike NAT gateway traffic, making this the most cost-effective solution for this specific S3/DynamoDB access pattern.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Adding more NAT gateways increases cost (more hourly charges) rather than reducing it.
Option C: NAT gateway bandwidth is not manually configured/billed this way, and this doesn’t reduce the underlying per-GB processing charges.
Option D: Moving instances to public subnets increases the security exposure and doesn’t address the cost question — it also isn’t necessary since Gateway endpoints solve the private-access requirement without public exposure.
Source: Gateway VPC endpoints

A company runs a Lambda function that has highly variable and unpredictable invocation volume, from zero invocations for hours to sudden bursts of thousands per second. The team wants to avoid paying for idle, pre-warmed capacity but is also concerned about cold-start latency during bursts.

Which statement about Lambda cost/performance tradeoffs is correct?

⬜ A. Lambda always charges a flat fee regardless of invocation count or duration, so cold starts have no cost implication.
✅ B. Provisioned Concurrency keeps a specified number of execution environments pre-initialized (eliminating cold starts for that many concurrent invocations) but incurs its own hourly charge whether or not it’s actively invoked — so it should be sized deliberately, not left at maximum ‘just in case,’ to avoid paying for unused pre-warmed capacity.
⬜ C. Reserved Concurrency and Provisioned Concurrency are the same feature with two different names.
⬜ D. Lambda cannot scale to handle bursts of thousands of concurrent invocations under any configuration.

Explanation:
Provisioned Concurrency pre-initializes a specified number of execution environments so invocations up to that number avoid cold-start latency — but AWS charges for Provisioned Concurrency by the hour it’s configured, regardless of whether it’s actually invoked during that time. This means over-provisioning it ‘just in case’ pays for idle capacity, which is exactly the cost/performance tradeoff the team needs to size deliberately (e.g., based on known traffic patterns) rather than maximizing blindly.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Lambda’s standard pricing model is based on the number of requests and the duration/memory of each invocation — it is not a flat fee, and cold starts (while not directly billed extra) do add latency that can matter for user-facing workloads.
Option C: Reserved Concurrency sets a maximum (and reserved) number of concurrent executions a function can use (a ceiling/guarantee, not a warm-pool mechanism); Provisioned Concurrency specifically pre-initializes environments to eliminate cold starts — they solve different problems.
Option D: Lambda is designed to scale automatically to handle large bursts of concurrent invocations, subject to account/function concurrency limits, which can be raised.
Source: Managing Lambda reserved concurrency / Configuring provisioned concurrency

A company runs a DynamoDB table whose traffic is highly unpredictable — some days see almost no requests, and other days see sudden, large spikes with no advance warning. The team has found it difficult to set provisioned capacity that’s both cost-efficient on quiet days and sufficient on spike days.

Which DynamoDB capacity mode BEST fits this workload?

⬜ A. Provisioned capacity mode with a fixed, high capacity setting sized for the largest expected spike, left unchanged at all times.
✅ B. DynamoDB On-Demand capacity mode, which automatically scales to accommodate traffic and charges per request actually made, with no capacity planning required.
⬜ C. Provisioned capacity mode with auto scaling disabled.
⬜ D. Reserved capacity purchased for a 3-year term.

Explanation:
DynamoDB On-Demand capacity mode automatically adapts to actual traffic in real time — instantly scaling up for spikes and down during quiet periods — and bills based on the read/write requests actually consumed, rather than requiring the team to provision (and pay for) capacity ahead of time. This is the best fit for workloads with unpredictable, spiky traffic where advance capacity planning is difficult.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Statically provisioning for the largest expected spike means paying for that much capacity around the clock, even on quiet days when it’s mostly unused — the opposite of cost-efficient.
Option C: Provisioned mode with auto scaling disabled would not adjust to spikes at all, risking throttling during bursts.
Option D: A 3-year reserved capacity commitment is best suited to steady, predictable, well-understood workloads — not the unpredictable, spiky pattern described here.
Source: Read/write capacity mode

A company runs an Amazon Aurora database that experiences highly variable load — near-zero traffic overnight and on weekends, with sharp spikes during business hours. The team wants to avoid paying for a fixed-capacity Aurora instance that sits mostly idle outside business hours, while still automatically scaling up seamlessly during peak load.

Which Aurora deployment option BEST fits this requirement?

⬜ A. A single fixed-size Aurora provisioned DB instance sized for peak business-hours load, running continuously.
✅ B. Amazon Aurora Serverless v2, which scales database capacity up and down automatically based on actual load, in fine-grained increments.
⬜ C. A larger, more expensive Aurora Multi-AZ cluster with no scaling configuration.
⬜ D. Manually resizing the Aurora instance class every morning and evening via a scheduled script.

Explanation:
Amazon Aurora Serverless v2 automatically and near-instantly adjusts database capacity in fine-grained increments based on actual application load, scaling down (reducing cost) during idle periods like nights and weekends and scaling up seamlessly during business-hour spikes — directly matching a workload with this kind of variable, on/off traffic pattern, without manual intervention.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: A fixed-size instance sized for peak load means paying for that peak capacity even during the many idle hours, which is not cost-efficient for this access pattern.
Option C: A larger, static Multi-AZ cluster with no scaling configuration doesn’t address cost efficiency during idle periods.
Option D: Manually resizing via a scheduled script adds operational complexity and risk (e.g., missed schedules, resize downtime) compared to Aurora Serverless v2’s automatic, seamless scaling.
Source: Aurora Serverless v2

A company serves a website’s static assets (images, CSS, JavaScript) directly from an Amazon S3 bucket to a global user base. Data transfer OUT costs from S3 directly to the internet have become a significant portion of the bill, and repeat requests for the same popular assets are common.

Which solution will MOST directly reduce this data transfer cost while also improving performance for users?

⬜ A. Move the assets to S3 Glacier for lower storage cost.
✅ B. Put an Amazon CloudFront distribution in front of the S3 bucket, so repeat requests are served from the CloudFront cache at edge locations instead of transferring data out of S3 on every single request.
⬜ C. Enable S3 Versioning on the bucket.
⬜ D. Increase the S3 bucket’s default storage class replication factor.

Explanation:
When CloudFront is placed in front of an S3 origin, popular content is cached at CloudFront edge locations, so repeat requests are served directly from the cache rather than triggering a new data transfer out of the S3 origin each time. Beyond the performance benefit of edge caching, data transferred from S3 to CloudFront (as an AWS-to-AWS transfer) and served from CloudFront’s cache is typically billed more favorably than direct-from-S3-to-internet transfer for repeat requests, reducing the origin’s outbound data transfer cost for popular, frequently-requested assets.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Moving frequently-accessed static assets used for a live website to Glacier would introduce retrieval delays/fees entirely unsuited to serving a website, and doesn’t address per-request transfer cost the same way caching does.
Option C: S3 Versioning protects against accidental deletion/overwrite; it has no relationship to data transfer cost.
Option D: S3 doesn’t have a user-configurable ‘replication factor’ setting in this sense, and this wouldn’t address data transfer cost.
Source: Amazon CloudFront pricing / Using Amazon S3 origins

A company generates a large volume of derivative data files each month (e.g., transcoded video renditions) that can always be regenerated from the original source files if lost, and that are rarely accessed after the first few days. The company wants the lowest-cost S3 storage option appropriate for this data, accepting that it will be stored in only a single Availability Zone.

Which S3 storage class is the BEST fit?

⬜ A. S3 Standard.
✅ B. S3 One Zone-IA.
⬜ C. S3 Standard-IA.
⬜ D. S3 Glacier Deep Archive, with no lifecycle transition — stored there from the moment of creation.

Explanation:
S3 One Zone-IA stores data in a single Availability Zone (rather than across multiple AZs) at a lower cost than S3 Standard-IA, which is an appropriate tradeoff specifically for data that is infrequently accessed AND easily reproducible if the AZ storing it were to be lost — exactly the scenario described (derivative, regenerable data).
Incorrect Options:
Option A: S3 Standard is priced for frequently-accessed data replicated across multiple AZs — more expensive than necessary for infrequently-accessed, reproducible data.
Option C: S3 Standard-IA offers the same infrequent-access pricing model but with multi-AZ redundancy, at a higher cost than One Zone-IA — appropriate when the data is NOT easily reproducible, but not the cheapest fit when it is.
Option D: Storing data in Glacier Deep Archive from the moment of creation would make even the ‘accessed within the first few days’ portion slow and costly to retrieve, which doesn’t fit the described access pattern.
Source: Using Amazon S3 storage classes

A company allocated several Elastic IP addresses to EC2 instances for a project, but some of those Elastic IPs are no longer associated with any running instance. The finance team notices a charge related to these addresses on the monthly bill.

Which statement correctly explains this charge?

⬜ A. Elastic IP addresses are always free, regardless of whether they’re associated with a running instance.
✅ B. AWS charges an hourly fee for an Elastic IP address that is allocated to your account but NOT associated with a running instance; an Elastic IP associated with a running instance is not charged this fee.
⬜ C. Elastic IP addresses are billed as a one-time purchase fee only, with no ongoing charge.
⬜ D. The charge must be unrelated to Elastic IPs, since AWS never charges for IP addresses.

Explanation:
AWS charges a small hourly fee specifically for Elastic IP addresses that are allocated to an account but not associated with a running instance (an ‘idle’ Elastic IP) — this is intended to encourage the efficient use of the limited pool of public IPv4 addresses. An Elastic IP that is actively associated with a running instance does not incur this particular idle-address charge.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Elastic IPs are not unconditionally free — the idle-address charge specifically applies when they’re not in active use.
Option C: The charge is an ongoing hourly rate while the address remains unassociated, not a one-time fee.
Option D: AWS does charge for unused/idle Elastic IP addresses specifically — this is a well-documented, real billing line item.
Source: Amazon EC2 pricing — Elastic IP addresses

A company is choosing between Amazon EBS gp2 and gp3 volumes for a workload that requires consistent 6,000 IOPS and 250 MB/s throughput, independent of the volume’s storage size. The team wants to understand the cost implication of this choice.

Which statement is correct?

✅ A. With gp2, IOPS scale with the size of the volume you provision (so you may need to over-provision storage capacity just to reach a target IOPS level); with gp3, IOPS and throughput can be provisioned independently of volume size, which can be more cost-effective when you need higher performance without needing more storage capacity.
⬜ B. gp2 and gp3 have identical pricing and performance characteristics in every respect.
⬜ C. gp3 volumes cannot exceed 3,000 IOPS under any circumstances.
⬜ D. IOPS and throughput are not billed separately from storage on either volume type.

Explanation:
On gp2 volumes, baseline IOPS are determined by the volume’s size (3 IOPS per GB, up to a cap), so reaching a specific IOPS target might require provisioning more storage than you actually need. gp3 volumes decouple IOPS and throughput from storage size — you get a baseline of 3,000 IOPS and 125 MB/s included, and can provision additional IOPS and throughput independently, up to their maximums, without having to over-provision storage capacity, which is often more cost-effective for performance-driven (rather than capacity-driven) requirements.
Incorrect Options:
Option B: gp2 and gp3 have different pricing structures and different ways of determining IOPS/throughput — they are not identical.
Option C: gp3 volumes support provisioning IOPS well above 3,000 (up to a much higher maximum) for an additional cost.
Option D: On gp3, provisioned IOPS and throughput above the baseline are billed as separate line items from the storage (GB) charge.
Source: Amazon EBS volume types — gp3

A company replicates data from an S3 bucket in one AWS Region to a bucket in another Region for compliance purposes, using S3 Cross-Region Replication (CRR). The team wants to understand what drives the cost of this replication beyond the storage cost of the destination bucket.

Which statement is correct?

⬜ A. Cross-Region Replication is entirely free; only the destination bucket’s storage cost applies.
✅ B. Cross-Region Replication incurs inter-Region data transfer charges for the replicated data (in addition to the destination bucket’s storage costs and any applicable request charges) — same-Region replication does not incur this inter-Region data transfer charge.
⬜ C. Replication cost is identical whether the destination bucket is in the same Region or a different Region.
⬜ D. AWS only charges for replication if the source bucket has fewer than 1,000 objects.

Explanation:
S3 Cross-Region Replication copies objects to a bucket in a different Region, and that cross-Region copy incurs standard inter-Region data transfer charges (on top of the destination bucket’s storage cost and PUT/COPY request charges) — a cost that does not apply to S3 Same-Region Replication (SRR), since data doesn’t leave the Region in that case.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Replication is not free — data transfer, storage, and request charges all apply.
Option C: Same-Region replication avoids the inter-Region data transfer charge that Cross-Region replication incurs, so the cost is not identical.
Option D: S3 replication charges are based on data volume and requests, not a fixed object-count threshold like this.
Source: Replication (Amazon S3) — costs

A company manages multiple AWS accounts under AWS Organizations for different departments. Each department is currently billed and pays for its own On-Demand usage independently, and the company has noticed it isn’t receiving the volume-tier pricing discounts that some AWS services offer at higher usage levels, even though the combined usage across all departments would qualify.

Which AWS Organizations feature addresses this?

⬜ A. Service control policies (SCPs).
✅ B. Consolidated billing, which aggregates usage across all member accounts in the organization so the combined usage can qualify for volume pricing tiers and Reserved Instance/Savings Plans sharing.
⬜ C. AWS Organizations tag policies.
⬜ D. Cross-account IAM roles.

Explanation:
AWS Organizations’ consolidated billing feature combines usage from all member accounts, which can help the combined organization reach volume pricing discount tiers (offered by some services based on aggregate usage) that no single account might reach alone, and also allows Reserved Instance and Savings Plans discounts purchased in one account to be shared across other accounts in the organization — directly addressing the described cost inefficiency.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: SCPs control what actions are permitted in member accounts; they have no effect on billing aggregation or volume discounts.
Option C: Tag policies standardize resource tagging for cost allocation reporting; they don’t themselves aggregate usage for volume pricing.
Option D: Cross-account IAM roles enable access delegation between accounts; they have no billing/volume-discount function.
Source: Consolidated billing for AWS Organizations

A company has committed to a 1-year Compute Savings Plan sized for its current EC2 usage. Over the next several months, the company expects to shift some workloads from the current instance family to a different, newer instance family, and possibly move some workloads to AWS Fargate.

Which statement about Savings Plans flexibility is correct?

⬜ A. A Compute Savings Plan commitment locks the company into the exact instance family and Region used at purchase time, with no flexibility.
✅ B. A Compute Savings Plan’s discount automatically applies to EC2 usage regardless of instance family, size, OS, or Region, AND also applies to AWS Fargate and AWS Lambda usage — making it well-suited to a company anticipating this kind of change.
⬜ C. Only EC2 Instance Savings Plans (not Compute Savings Plans) can ever apply to Fargate usage.
⬜ D. Savings Plans cannot be combined with On-Demand usage in any way — a workload must be 100% covered by the Savings Plan commitment or receive no discount at all.

Explanation:
A Compute Savings Plan provides the most flexibility among Savings Plans/RI options: its discount (in exchange for a committed $/hour spend) automatically applies to EC2 usage across any instance family, size, OS, tenancy, and Region, and also extends to AWS Fargate and AWS Lambda usage — making it the right choice when future compute needs (like changing instance families or adopting Fargate) are not yet fully certain.
Incorrect Options:
