Option A: This describes EC2 Instance Savings Plans (or Standard Reserved Instances), which are tied to a specific instance family and Region — not the more flexible Compute Savings Plan.
Option C: Compute Savings Plans (not just EC2 Instance Savings Plans) are the ones that extend to Fargate and Lambda usage.
Option D: Savings Plans apply their discount up to the committed hourly spend, and any usage beyond that commitment is simply billed at standard On-Demand rates — the two coexist rather than being all-or-nothing.
Source: How Savings Plans apply to your AWS usage

A company runs stateless batch-processing jobs on Amazon EC2 that can tolerate individual instances being interrupted and restarted, as long as the overall job eventually completes. The company wants the deepest possible discount on compute while maintaining reasonable availability of capacity, and is open to using a diversified mix of instance types to reduce the chance of losing all capacity at once.

Which EC2 purchasing approach is the BEST fit?

⬜ A. On-Demand Instances of a single instance type only.
✅ B. A Spot Fleet (or EC2 Auto Scaling group with a mixed instances policy) configured with several diversified Spot Instance types/pools, using the ‘capacity-optimized’ allocation strategy.
⬜ C. A single large Dedicated Host.
⬜ D. Standard Reserved Instances with a 3-year all-upfront commitment.

Explanation:
Spot Instances offer the deepest discount off On-Demand pricing (often 70-90%) and are well-suited to interruption-tolerant, stateless batch workloads. Diversifying across several instance types/pools — via a Spot Fleet or an Auto Scaling group’s mixed instances policy with the capacity-optimized allocation strategy — reduces the chance of losing all capacity simultaneously (since interruptions are more likely to be correlated within a single instance pool), improving effective availability while still capturing Spot’s cost savings.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: On-Demand pricing is the most expensive per-hour option, and a single instance type provides no diversification against interruption/capacity constraints (though On-Demand isn’t interrupted by AWS the way Spot is, that’s not the primary cost concern here).
Option C: A Dedicated Host is priced for hardware isolation/licensing use cases, not for maximizing discount on interruption-tolerant batch work — it’s also generally more expensive than Spot.
Option D: A 3-year Reserved Instance commitment doesn’t fit a workload explicitly described as tolerant of interruption where the goal is the deepest possible discount — Spot achieves a steeper discount for this specific workload profile.
Source: Spot Fleet allocation strategies

A research organization hosts a large public dataset in an Amazon S3 bucket that external researchers around the world download from. The organization wants external users to be able to access the data, but does not want to bear the full data transfer cost of every download itself.

Which S3 feature allows the organization to shift the data transfer and request costs to the users downloading the data, instead of the bucket owner?

⬜ A. S3 Object Lock.
✅ B. S3 Requester Pays.
⬜ C. S3 Transfer Acceleration.
⬜ D. S3 Same-Region Replication.

Explanation:
When S3 Requester Pays is enabled on a bucket, the requester (the person or account downloading the object), rather than the bucket owner, is charged for the data transfer and request costs of accessing the objects — a common pattern for organizations that host large public datasets and want to avoid absorbing the full download cost themselves, while still making the data broadly accessible.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Object Lock provides WORM (write-once-read-many) protection against deletion; it has no relationship to who pays for data transfer.
Option C: Transfer Acceleration speeds up uploads/downloads over long distances via CloudFront edge locations; it doesn’t change who is billed.
Option D: Same-Region Replication copies objects to another bucket in the same Region; it doesn’t affect who pays for a given download request.
Source: Requester Pays buckets

A company runs an Amazon Redshift cluster for business intelligence reporting that operates at a fairly steady, predictable size year-round. The company wants to reduce its Redshift compute costs compared to On-Demand pricing, given this steady, well-understood usage pattern.

Which purchasing option should the company use?

⬜ D. Amazon Athena instead of Redshift, since Athena has no compute cost whatsoever.

Explanation:
Amazon Redshift Reserved Nodes let a company commit to a specific node type/quantity for a 1- or 3-year term in exchange for a significantly discounted hourly rate compared to On-Demand pricing — the ideal purchasing option for a steady, predictable, well-understood cluster size, similar in principle to EC2 Reserved Instances.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: On-Demand pricing, while flexible, is the most expensive per-hour rate — not cost-optimal for a workload with steady, predictable usage that’s well suited to a reservation.
Option C: Redshift Spectrum lets you query data directly in S3 without loading it into the cluster, complementing (not replacing) a provisioned cluster for this BI workload, and doesn’t itself represent a purchasing commitment for the existing cluster’s compute.
Option D: Amazon Athena does have its own (per-query-scanned) pricing — it is not free — and switching engines entirely is a major architectural change, not simply a cost-optimization purchasing decision for the existing Redshift workload.

A company’s Amazon CloudWatch Logs are configured with no expiration (Never Expire) for every log group, across dozens of applications. The storage cost for CloudWatch Logs has been steadily increasing month over month, and most log groups are never queried after the first 30 days.

Which change will MOST directly reduce this growing cost, without losing any ability to review logs from the last month?

✅ A. Set a log retention policy on each log group (e.g., 30 days) so that CloudWatch Logs automatically expires and deletes older log events.
⬜ B. Increase the memory allocated to the Lambda functions writing the logs.
⬜ C. Switch from CloudWatch Logs to Amazon S3 Standard storage for all future logging, with no other changes.
⬜ D. Disable CloudWatch Logs entirely.

Explanation:
CloudWatch Logs supports a configurable retention policy per log group — setting it to, for example, 30 days causes CloudWatch to automatically expire and delete log events older than that threshold, directly controlling the ever-growing storage cost, while still preserving full access to the logs from the period the company actually reviews (the last 30 days).
Incorrect Options:
Option B: Lambda memory allocation affects compute cost/performance, not CloudWatch Logs storage retention or cost.
Option C: Simply pointing future logs at S3 doesn’t address the cost already accumulating for existing CloudWatch Logs log groups with no expiration, and moving logging destinations entirely is a bigger architectural change than necessary.
Option D: Disabling CloudWatch Logs entirely removes the ability to review recent logs too, which the company still wants for the last 30 days.

A company wants to be automatically notified as soon as its actual or forecasted AWS spend for the month is on track to exceed a specific dollar threshold, without waiting until the monthly bill arrives to find out.

⬜ D. Amazon CloudWatch Logs Insights.

AWS Budgets lets a company define a cost (or usage) budget with a specific threshold, and configure alerts — including via Amazon SNS notifications — that trigger automatically when actual spend, or AWS’s forecasted spend, is on track to exceed that threshold, giving proactive, automated warning well before the bill arrives, rather than requiring a manual check.

Option C: Trusted Advisor’s performance checks are unrelated to cost-threshold alerting.
Option D: CloudWatch Logs Insights is a log query tool, not a cost-monitoring or budget-alerting service.

An Amazon EC2 Auto Scaling group is configured with a fixed desired capacity that never changes, sized for peak daily traffic. The application’s actual traffic is much lower for roughly 16 hours a day (nights and off-peak periods), leaving significant compute capacity idle and unnecessarily increasing costs.

Which change will MOST directly reduce this cost while still meeting peak demand when it occurs?

⬜ A. Replace the Auto Scaling group with a single, larger EC2 instance running at all times.
✅ B. Configure a dynamic (e.g., target tracking) or scheduled scaling policy so the Auto Scaling group’s capacity automatically decreases during predictable low-traffic periods and increases again for peak periods.
⬜ C. Increase the fixed desired capacity even further to add a safety margin.
⬜ D. Switch all instances in the group to a larger, more expensive instance type.

Explanation:
Adding a dynamic scaling policy (such as target tracking based on CPU utilization or request count) or a scheduled scaling policy (if the low-traffic hours are predictable) lets the Auto Scaling group automatically scale capacity down during the ~16 idle hours and back up for peak demand — directly reducing the cost of running fixed, peak-sized capacity around the clock, while still meeting demand when traffic actually rises.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: A single larger always-on instance still runs at (or near) capacity all the time, without the elasticity to shrink during idle periods.
Option C: Increasing the already-fixed desired capacity would increase cost further, the opposite of the goal.
Option D: Larger instance types increase per-hour cost without introducing any elasticity to match the actual variable demand.

A company needs to archive compliance records that must be retrievable, but typically only once or twice a year, and retrieval within roughly 12 hours is acceptable. The company compared S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval and S3 Glacier Deep Archive and wants to choose the more cost-effective option given this specific access pattern.

Which factor should drive the decision?

⬜ A. Glacier Deep Archive has a higher storage cost than Glacier Flexible Retrieval, so Flexible Retrieval is always the better choice regardless of retrieval speed needs.
✅ B. Glacier Deep Archive offers the lowest storage cost of any S3 storage class, but has a longer standard retrieval time (typically within 12 hours) than Glacier Flexible Retrieval — since the company’s 12-hour retrieval tolerance fits Deep Archive’s standard retrieval tier, Deep Archive is likely the more cost-effective choice for this rarely-accessed, retrieval-time-tolerant data.

⬜ D. Neither storage class supports retrieval times under 48 hours, so both are equally unsuitable.

Explanation:
S3 Glacier Deep Archive is designed for the lowest storage cost among S3 storage classes, intended for data accessed roughly once or twice a year, with a standard retrieval time of about 12 hours. Since the company’s stated retrieval tolerance (about 12 hours) aligns with Deep Archive’s standard tier, and its access frequency (once or twice yearly) matches Deep Archive’s intended use case, it’s likely the more cost-effective choice compared to Glacier Flexible Retrieval, which costs more but offers faster retrieval options the company doesn’t need here.

A company with many AWS accounts under AWS Organizations wants the finance team to be able to see exactly how much each of several internal projects costs, even when resources for different projects run in the same account.

Which approach enables cost visibility at the individual-project level, rather than only at the account level?

⬜ A. Rely solely on account-level billing, since cost visibility below the account level isn’t possible in AWS.
✅ B. Apply consistent cost allocation tags (e.g., a ‘project’ tag) to resources, activate those tags in the Billing console, and use AWS Cost Explorer / Cost and Usage Reports filtered/grouped by that tag.

AWS supports cost allocation tags — user-defined tags (like ‘project’ or ‘cost-center’) applied consistently to resources — which, once activated in the Billing and Cost Management console, allow cost data to be broken down, filtered, and grouped by those tag values in Cost Explorer and Cost and Usage Reports. Incorrect Options:

Option C: SCPs control permissions, not cost visibility or billing granularity.
Option D: AWS Config tracks resource configuration compliance; it doesn’t provide cost breakdowns.

A company is launching a new containerized application and wants to run it on AWS without provisioning, patching, or managing any underlying EC2 servers or a Kubernetes/ECS cluster’s control plane, while paying only for the vCPU and memory resources the containers actually use.

Which compute option BEST minimizes both operational overhead and cost for this requirement?

⬜ A. Amazon EC2 instances running Docker, manually patched and scaled by the team.
✅ B. AWS Fargate, used as the launch type for Amazon ECS or Amazon EKS, so containers run without the company managing any underlying servers.
⬜ C. A self-managed Kubernetes cluster on Amazon EC2 with manually provisioned worker nodes.
⬜ D. A single large, always-on Amazon EC2 instance running all containers simultaneously with no orchestration.

Explanation:
AWS Fargate is a serverless compute engine for containers that works with both Amazon ECS and Amazon EKS — it removes the need to provision, patch, or scale the underlying EC2 instances (or manage cluster capacity) entirely, and bills based on the vCPU and memory resources the containers actually request/use, directly minimizing both operational overhead (no servers to manage) and cost (no idle, over-provisioned EC2 capacity) for this requirement.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Running Docker on manually managed EC2 instances still requires the team to provision, patch, and scale the underlying servers — the opposite of minimal operational overhead.
Option C: A self-managed Kubernetes cluster with manually provisioned worker nodes still requires managing EC2 capacity, patching, and cluster operations directly.
Option D: A single always-on instance with no orchestration provides neither the operational automation nor the elastic, pay-for-what-you-use cost model of Fargate.

A company has dozens of AWS accounts and wants an automated, ongoing way to surface cost-saving opportunities, such as idle load balancers, underutilized EC2 instances, and unattached EBS volumes, without writing custom scripts.

✅ A. AWS Trusted Advisor cost optimization checks.

AWS Trusted Advisor inspects an account’s AWS environment and provides real-time recommendations across several categories, including cost optimization — flagging idle load balancers, low-utilization EC2 instances, unassociated Elastic IP addresses, and unattached/underutilized EBS volumes, among others — giving an automated, ongoing way to find savings opportunities without custom scripting.
Incorrect Options:

Option C: CloudWatch Logs Insights is a log query/analysis tool, not a cost-optimization recommendation engine.
Option D: AWS Config conformance packs check resource configuration compliance against rules; they are not focused on cost optimization.
Source: AWS Trusted Advisor

An analytics team runs an EC2 instance for interactive, in-memory data exploration. Between sessions (often overnight or over a weekend), the instance is not used at all, but restarting the analysis from a completely cold instance (reloading data into memory, reopening files) takes a long time. The team wants to avoid paying for compute while idle, but also avoid the slow cold-start reload each time.

Which EC2 feature BEST meets this need?

⬜ A. Stop the instance when idle, since a stopped instance always resumes with all in-memory state intact.
✅ B. Hibernate the instance when idle: this stops billing for instance (compute) usage while saving the in-memory (RAM) state to the root EBS volume, so the session resumes quickly with memory state restored on start.
⬜ C. Leave the instance running continuously to avoid any loss of in-memory state.
⬜ D. Terminate the instance after each session and launch a brand-new instance for the next session.

Explanation:
EC2 hibernation stops billing for instance usage the same way a normal stop does (no compute charges while hibernated, only EBS storage charges continue), but before stopping, it saves the contents of RAM to the root EBS volume. When the instance is started again, EC2 restores that saved RAM contents, so the OS boot and in-memory application state resume much faster than a cold start, without paying for compute while idle.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: A normal stop discards the contents of RAM; only hibernation preserves in-memory state across a stop/start cycle.
Option C: Leaving the instance running continuously incurs full compute charges even while completely idle, which is exactly what the team wants to avoid.
Option D: Terminating and relaunching a fresh instance each time loses all in-memory state and requires a full cold start and data reload every session — worse than hibernation for this need.
Source: Hibernate your Amazon EC2 instance

An application in a private VPC subnet (no internet access) needs to call the Amazon S3 API frequently, moving large volumes of data. The company currently routes this traffic through a NAT gateway to reach the public S3 endpoint and has noticed this adds meaningful cost.

Which change would MOST reduce the cost of this specific traffic pattern?

⬜ A. Replace the NAT gateway with a NAT instance of the same size.
✅ B. Add a gateway VPC endpoint for Amazon S3, so traffic to S3 no longer needs to traverse the NAT gateway at all.
⬜ C. Add an interface VPC endpoint (powered by AWS PrivateLink) for Amazon S3, which bills hourly plus per-GB but avoids the NAT gateway.
⬜ D. Increase the NAT gateway’s assigned Elastic IP allocation.

Explanation:
A gateway VPC endpoint for Amazon S3 (and DynamoDB) routes traffic to those services privately within the VPC via route table entries, without going through a NAT gateway or the internet at all — and gateway endpoints themselves have no hourly or per-GB charge. This eliminates both the NAT gateway’s per-GB data processing charge and the need for NAT entirely for this traffic, making it the most cost-effective fix for high-volume S3 traffic from a private subnet.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: A same-sized NAT instance still incurs per-GB-equivalent data transfer/instance costs and doesn’t eliminate the underlying cost driver the way removing NAT from the path does.
Option C: An interface endpoint (AWS PrivateLink) does work for services that don’t support gateway endpoints, but it bills an hourly charge plus per-GB data processing charge — for S3 specifically, the free gateway endpoint is the more cost-effective option.
Option D: Elastic IP allocation has no bearing on NAT gateway data processing charges.
Source: VPC endpoints for Amazon S3

An organization is currently operating MySQL databases on its own on-premises servers. To reduce the operational burden of database maintenance and management, the organization wants to move to a fully managed AWS database offering. Which migration strategy best aligns with this goal?

⬜ A. Migrate the databases to self-managed MySQL running on Amazon EC2 instances, keeping full control over OS and database patching.
✅ B. Use AWS Database Migration Service (AWS DMS) to migrate the on-premises MySQL databases to Amazon RDS for MySQL, a fully managed relational database service.
⬜ C. Export the data to Amazon S3 as flat files and query it directly with Amazon Athena instead of using a relational database.
⬜ D. Replicate the on-premises MySQL servers into a second on-premises data center for redundancy, without involving AWS.

Explanation:
AWS Database Migration Service (AWS DMS) can migrate an on-premises MySQL database, including ongoing changes via continuous replication, into Amazon RDS for MySQL — a fully managed offering where AWS handles patching, backups, and infrastructure maintenance, directly reducing the organization’s operational burden.
Incorrect Options:
Option A: Running MySQL on self-managed EC2 instances still requires the organization to patch the OS and database itself, so it doesn’t reduce operational burden the way a fully managed service does.
Option C: Moving relational, transactional data into flat files queried by Athena changes the data model entirely and isn’t a like-for-like migration to a managed relational database.
Option D: Replicating between on-premises data centers doesn’t move any workload to AWS or reduce the organization’s own database management burden.
Source: AWS Database Migration Service User Guide
